People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Well… it’s been since my wife’s fall, really. I used to be the one with lists, schedules, the plan for everything — I was always organized. I even used to help younger people with mentoring calls, you know, keep everyone on track.

But now I just sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and I’ll end up crying or snapping over the smallest thing. The bills are piling up, housework isn’t getting done, I miss calls… it’s like I can’t make myself function the way I used to.

I haven’t been paying attention to it in that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit with a cup of tea, and for a moment I think, yes, this is something normal — but then I notice the mug has that chip in it from years ago, and I remember we bought that set on a trip to the coast. Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.

I used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. Then the bills sit there, the mentoring calls don’t happen, the housework piles up… and I just can’t seem to get myself moving.

It’s strange, because I always prided myself on being practical. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.

I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. Mentoring, especially — I used to show up for that regularly, and the community meetings too. I liked being useful, you know. There was a young fellow I was helping once who brought the most terrible biscuits to every session, dry as cardboard, but he meant well.

But now… I’ve stopped going, mostly. I don’t really keep up with those commitments. And it’s not just that. Bills, little jobs around the house, keeping things ticking over — I used to do all that without even thinking. Now it all slips. The post piles up, something needs fixing and I just… don’t get to it. Even ordinary routines at home have gone rather badly off track.

I… I don’t quite know how to answer that neatly. It’s all been rather overwhelming since the fall, and some days my head feels full of fog.

I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.

I haven’t been paying attention to it in that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit with a cup of tea, and for a moment I think, yes, this is something normal — but then I notice the mug has that chip in it from years ago, and I remember we bought that set on a trip to the coast. Lovely little place, terrible parking, but the fish was good. My wife always said I rushed through meals there.\n\nSo… pleasure? I can’t say. I haven’t really noticed anything clearly enough to tell you yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know if that’s the right word. I keep going over things in my head, but then I’ll get caught on some silly detail, like where I’d left my glasses that morning or whether the hallway light had been flickering again. We had that light fixed once by a chap who talked nonstop about cricket — never did quite fix it properly, mind you.\n\nBut about guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.\n\nI used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. Then the bills sit there, the mentoring calls don’t happen, the housework piles up… and I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nIt’s strange, because I always prided myself on being practical. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still participate in, even if you don't feel pleasure from them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. Mentoring, especially — I used to show up for that regularly, and the community meetings too. I liked being useful, you know. There was a young fellow I was helping once who brought the most terrible biscuits to every session, dry as cardboard, but he meant well.\n\nBut now… I’ve stopped going, mostly. I don’t really keep up with those commitments. And it’s not just that. Bills, little jobs around the house, keeping things ticking over — I used to do all that without even thinking. Now it all slips. The post piles up, something needs fixing and I just… don’t get to it. Even ordinary routines at home have gone rather badly off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight. The scale’s in the bathroom cupboard somewhere, unless it got moved when we had that leak under the sink — awful business, water everywhere, and the plumber was late by hours.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. My clothes… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. I just haven’t properly checked.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t quite know how to answer that neatly. It’s all been rather overwhelming since the fall, and some days my head feels full of fog.\n\nHarming myself… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made any sort of plan, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just want everything to stop for a while — the forms, the appointments, the ringing phone. Yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for the insurance papers and found an old holiday brochure instead, from when we went to Dorset. Funny how things turn up like that.\n\nBut, sorry… your question. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.\n\nBut wishing I wouldn’t wake up… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve had that as a clear thought. It’s more that I dread the day starting sometimes — the kettle, the letters on the mat, trying to remember who’s meant to be coming round. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.

I suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.

But no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.

I suppose I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying attention to it in that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit with a cup of tea, and for a moment I think, yes, this is something normal — but then I notice the mug has that chip in it from years ago, and I remember we bought that set on a trip to the coast. Lovely little place, terrible parking, but the fish was good. My wife always said I rushed through meals there.\n\nSo… pleasure? I can’t say. I haven’t really noticed anything clearly enough to tell you yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know if that’s the right word. I keep going over things in my head, but then I’ll get caught on some silly detail, like where I’d left my glasses that morning or whether the hallway light had been flickering again. We had that light fixed once by a chap who talked nonstop about cricket — never did quite fix it properly, mind you.\n\nBut about guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I am. It’s hard not to be. Since her fall, everything in the house seems to point back to her — her medication chart on the kitchen table, the cushion she likes, even the little notes she used to leave by the phone.

I used to be the one with lists, you know. I had a plan for everything. My old students used to tease me about it when I was mentoring — “Have you got a spreadsheet for that?” they’d say. And now I just sit there staring at that chart, and I’ll either start crying or snap over something ridiculous, like a bill coming through the letterbox.

So yes, I’m preoccupied with her, but it’s not just thinking of her fondly. It’s like everything’s got stuck there, around the fall, and the rest of life — bills, calls, the washing-up, the hoovering — it all piles up because I can’t seem to get myself moving.

I haven’t really noticed anything clearly enough to tell you yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know if that’s the right word. I keep going over things in my head, but then I’ll get caught on some silly detail, like where I’d left my glasses that morning or whether the hallway light had been flickering again. We had that light fixed once by a chap who talked nonstop about cricket — never did quite fix it properly, mind you.\n\nBut about guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.\n\nI used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. Then the bills sit there, the mentoring calls don’t happen, the housework piles up… and I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nIt’s strange, because I always prided myself on being practical. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still participate in, even if you don't feel pleasure from them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.\n\nBut wishing I wouldn’t wake up… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve had that as a clear thought. It’s more that I dread the day starting sometimes — the kettle, the letters on the mat, trying to remember who’s meant to be coming round. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.\n\nI suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.\n\nI used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still participate in, even if you don't feel pleasure from them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. There was a young fellow I was helping once who brought the most terrible biscuits to every session, dry as cardboard, but he meant well.\n\nBut now… I’ve stopped going, mostly. I couldn’t honestly say it’s been significant, no. I haven’t put myself on the scales, and I suppose “significant” sounds like something you’d measure properly, doesn’t it?

I mean, my belt’s the same hole, as far as I’ve noticed — though I’ve had that belt for years, leather all cracked near the buckle. My wife always said I should buy a new one, but I never liked shopping for that sort of thing. You go in for one belt and come out wondering why there are twenty types of black.

So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed losing a lot of weight.

I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly.

There are times I’ve sat there and thought, “What’s the point of all this?” You know, the house is so quiet in the evenings, and the clock in the hall sounds louder than it ever used to — we bought that clock on a day trip years ago, actually, she said it was ugly and then wouldn’t let me get rid of it.

Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.\n\nI suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your wife since her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. My clothes haven’t shouted at me, if that makes sense. I’m not suddenly swimming in them or anything I’ve properly noticed. Most days, yes… I think that’s fair to say.

Since my wife’s fall, I’ve just not been myself. I used to be the one with lists, plans, little systems for everything — bills in one folder, calls written down, shopping done on Tuesdays, that sort of thing. Now I sit at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and just… go to pieces sometimes.

And then other times I snap over nothing, which isn’t like me. The post piles up, housework piles up, things I said I’d do for people — mentoring calls and that — I just let them slide. So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.

Lovely little place, terrible parking, but the fish was good. My wife always said I rushed through meals there.\n\nSo… pleasure? I can’t say. I haven’t really noticed anything clearly enough to tell you yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know if that’s the right word. I keep going over things in my head, but then I’ll get caught on some silly detail, like where I’d left my glasses that morning or whether the hallway light had been flickering again. We had that light fixed once by a chap who talked nonstop about cricket — never did quite fix it properly, mind you.\n\nBut about guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.\n\nI used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. Then the bills sit there, the mentoring calls don’t happen, the housework piles up… and I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nIt’s strange, because I always prided myself on being practical. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still participate in, even if you don't feel pleasure from them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. Mentoring, especially — I used to show up for that regularly, and the community meetings too. I liked being useful, you know. There was a young fellow I was helping once who brought the most terrible biscuits to every session, dry as cardboard, but he meant well.\n\nBut now… I’ve stopped going, mostly. I don’t really keep up with those commitments. And it’s not just that. Bills, little jobs around the house, keeping things ticking over — I used to do all that without even thinking. Now it all slips. The post piles up, something needs fixing and I just… don’t get to it. Even ordinary routines at home have gone rather badly off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight. The scale’s in the bathroom cupboard somewhere, unless it got moved when we had that leak under the sink — awful business, water everywhere, and the plumber was late by hours.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. My clothes… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. I just haven’t properly checked.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t quite know how to answer that neatly. It’s all been rather overwhelming since the fall, and some days my head feels full of fog.\n\nHarming myself… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made any sort of plan, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just want everything to stop for a while — the forms, the appointments, the ringing phone. Yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for the insurance papers and found an old holiday brochure instead, from when we went to Dorset. Funny how things turn up like that.\n\nBut, sorry… your question. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.\n\nBut wishing I wouldn’t wake up… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve had that as a clear thought. It’s more that I dread the day starting sometimes — the kettle, the letters on the mat, trying to remember who’s meant to be coming round. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.\n\nI suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your wife since her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. It’s hard not to be. Since her fall, everything in the house seems to point back to her — her medication chart on the kitchen table, the cushion she likes, even the little notes she used to leave by the phone.\n\nI used to be the one with lists, you know. I had a plan for everything. My old students used to tease me about it when I was mentoring — “Have you got a spreadsheet for that?” they’d say. And now I just sit there staring at that chart, and I’ll either start crying or snap over something ridiculous, like a bill coming through the letterbox.\n\nSo yes, I’m preoccupied with her, but it’s not just thinking of her fondly. It’s like everything’s got stuck there, around the fall, and the rest of life — bills, calls, the washing-up, the hoovering — it all piles up because I can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t weighed myself. We’ve got scales somewhere, I think in the bathroom cupboard, unless my wife moved them—she used to tidy things into places that made perfect sense to her and none to me.\n\nAs for noticeable weight… I can’t really say. My clothes haven’t shouted at me, if that makes sense. I’m not suddenly swimming in them or anything I’ve properly noticed. But I haven’t been paying much attention to myself, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I couldn’t honestly say it’s been significant, no. I haven’t put myself on the scales, and I suppose “significant” sounds like something you’d measure properly, doesn’t it?\n\nI mean, my belt’s the same hole, as far as I’ve noticed — though I’ve had that belt for years, leather all cracked near the buckle. My wife always said I should buy a new one, but I never liked shopping for that sort of thing. You go in for one belt and come out wondering why there are twenty types of black.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed losing a lot of weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly.\n\nThere are times I’ve sat there and thought, “What’s the point of all this?” You know, the house is so quiet in the evenings, and the clock in the hall sounds louder than it ever used to — we bought that clock on a day trip years ago, actually, she said it was ugly and then wouldn’t let me get rid of it.\n\nBut if you mean have I properly thought about not being alive… I don’t know. It frightens me a bit even hearing it said like that. I haven’t really… I haven’t sorted it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I think that’s fair to say.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve just not been myself. I used to be the one with lists, plans, little systems for everything — bills in one folder, calls written down, shopping done on Tuesdays, that sort of thing. Now I sit at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and just… go to pieces sometimes.\n\nAnd then other times I snap over nothing, which isn’t like me. The post piles up, housework piles up, things I said I’d do for people — mentoring calls and that — I just let them slide. So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F43.21", "F43.23", "F32.0", "F32.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure… “lost interest” is a tricky way to put it.

There are things I haven’t done, certainly. I used to fuss over the garden a bit — not properly, mind you, more moving weeds from one place to another and pretending it was horticulture. My neighbour once told me my roses were “brave,” which I’m still not sure was a compliment.

But whether that means I don’t care about things anymore, or whether I’m just… distracted by everything since her fall, I don’t know. I still notice things. I still think I ought to do them. It’s just hard to say if the interest has gone, exactly.

I haven’t been paying attention to it in that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit with a cup of tea, and for a moment I think, yes, this is something normal — but then I notice the mug has that chip in it from years ago, and I remember we bought that set on a trip to the coast. I haven’t really noticed anything clearly enough to tell you yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know if that’s the right word. I keep going over things in my head, but then I’ll get caught on some silly detail, like where I’d left my glasses that morning or whether the hallway light had been flickering again. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.\n\nI used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still participate in, even if you don't feel pleasure from them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. Mentoring, especially — I used to show up for that regularly, and the community meetings too. I liked being useful, you know. There was a young fellow I was helping once who brought the most terrible biscuits to every session, dry as cardboard, but he meant well.\n\nBut now… I’ve stopped going, mostly. I don’t really keep up with those commitments. And it’s not just that. Bills, little jobs around the house, keeping things ticking over — I used to do all that without even thinking. Now it all slips. The post piles up, something needs fixing and I just… don’t get to it. Even ordinary routines at home have gone rather badly off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight. The scale’s in the bathroom cupboard somewhere, unless it got moved when we had that leak under the sink — awful business, water everywhere, and the plumber was late by hours.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. My clothes… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. I just haven’t properly checked.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t quite know how to answer that neatly. It’s all been rather overwhelming since the fall, and some days my head feels full of fog.\n\nHarming myself… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made any sort of plan, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just want everything to stop for a while — the forms, the appointments, the ringing phone. Yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for the insurance papers and found an old holiday brochure instead, from when we went to Dorset. Funny how things turn up like that.\n\nBut, sorry… your question. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.\n\nBut wishing I wouldn’t wake up… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve had that as a clear thought. It’s more that I dread the day starting sometimes — the kettle, the letters on the mat, trying to remember who’s meant to be coming round. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.\n\nI suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your wife since her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. It’s hard not to be. Since her fall, everything in the house seems to point back to her — her medication chart on the kitchen table, the cushion she likes, even the little notes she used to leave by the phone.\n\nI used to be the one with lists, you know. I had a plan for everything. My old students used to tease me about it when I was mentoring — “Have you got a spreadsheet for that?” they’d say. It’s like everything’s got stuck there, around the fall, and the rest of life — bills, calls, the washing-up, the hoovering — it all piles up because I can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t weighed myself. thinking—bills, home upkeep, and basic household routines—are now slipping badly.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two and then wouldn’t let me get rid of it.\n\nBut if you mean have I properly thought about not being alive… I don’t know. I haven’t really… I haven’t sorted it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I think that’s fair to say.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve just not been myself. I used to be the one with lists, plans, little systems for everything — bills in one folder, calls written down, shopping done on Tuesdays, that sort of thing. Now I sit at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and just… go to pieces sometimes.\n\nAnd then other times I snap over nothing, which isn’t like me. The post piles up, housework piles up, things I said I’d do for people — mentoring calls and that — I just let them slide. So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… “lost interest” is a tricky way to put it.\n\nThere are things I haven’t done, certainly. I used to fuss over the garden a bit — not properly, mind you, more moving weeds from one place to another and pretending it was horticulture. My neighbour once told me my roses were “brave,” which I’m still not sure was a compliment.\n\nBut whether that means I don’t care about things anymore, or whether I’m just… distracted by everything since her fall, I don’t know. I still notice things. I still think I ought to do them. It’s just hard to say if the interest has gone, exactly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your clothes are looser on you than they were before your wife's fall?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know, to be honest.

I haven’t noticed my clothes particularly hanging off me or anything like that. Mind you, I tend to wear the same old jumper most days at home — the blue one my wife always said made me look like I was about to fix a boiler, though I’ve never fixed a boiler in my life. I did once flood the airing cupboard trying to be useful, which she never let me forget.

But no… I can’t say I’ve really paid attention to whether things are looser. I haven’t noticed it, anyway.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.23, F32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it’s been since my wife’s fall, really. I used to be the one with lists, schedules, the plan for everything — I was always organized. I even used to help younger people with mentoring calls, you know, keep everyone on track.\n\nBut now I just sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and I’ll end up crying or snapping over the smallest thing. The bills are piling up, housework isn’t getting done, I miss calls… it’s like I can’t make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: About two months now, I think. Since her fall, really — everything at home just changed overnight. It’s funny, I used to mark dates on the calendar for birthdays and appointments, little reminders stuck everywhere… but this one, I don’t even need a note for it. It’s just been hanging over the house for about two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, like mentoring calls?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I haven’t really kept track of what I enjoy lately. The mentoring calls… well, I used to like feeling useful, you know? There was one young fellow years ago who reminded me of myself at that age, always rushing, tie crooked, thought he could solve everything with a spreadsheet.\n\nBut whether I get pleasure from it now… I don’t know. I suppose I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying attention to it in that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit with a cup of tea, and for a moment I think, yes, this is something normal — but then I notice the mug has that chip in it from years ago, and I remember we bought that set on a trip to the coast. Lovely little place, terrible parking, but the fish was good. My wife always said I rushed through meals there.\n\nSo… pleasure? I can’t say. I haven’t really noticed anything clearly enough to tell you yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know if that’s the right word. I keep going over things in my head, but then I’ll get caught on some silly detail, like where I’d left my glasses that morning or whether the hallway light had been flickering again. We had that light fixed once by a chap who talked nonstop about cricket — never did quite fix it properly, mind you.\n\nBut about guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really sorted out what I feel about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, I suppose I am, most of the time. Since my wife’s fall, I don’t feel like myself at all.\n\nI used to be the one with lists and plans — bills sorted, calls scheduled, everything in its place. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over some tiny thing. Then the bills sit there, the mentoring calls don’t happen, the housework piles up… and I just can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nIt’s strange, because I always prided myself on being practical. My old manager used to say I could turn chaos into a spreadsheet. Now even loading the dishwasher feels like too much some days. So yes… sad, and stuck, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still participate in, even if you don't feel pleasure from them?\n\nPatient: I suppose… there are things I used to enjoy, yes. Mentoring, especially — I used to show up for that regularly, and the community meetings too. I liked being useful, you know. There was a young fellow I was helping once who brought the most terrible biscuits to every session, dry as cardboard, but he meant well.\n\nBut now… I’ve stopped going, mostly. I don’t really keep up with those commitments. And it’s not just that. Bills, little jobs around the house, keeping things ticking over — I used to do all that without even thinking. Now it all slips. The post piles up, something needs fixing and I just… don’t get to it. Even ordinary routines at home have gone rather badly off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight. The scale’s in the bathroom cupboard somewhere, unless it got moved when we had that leak under the sink — awful business, water everywhere, and the plumber was late by hours.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. My clothes… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. I just haven’t properly checked.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t quite know how to answer that neatly. It’s all been rather overwhelming since the fall, and some days my head feels full of fog.\n\nHarming myself… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made any sort of plan, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just want everything to stop for a while — the forms, the appointments, the ringing phone. Yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for the insurance papers and found an old holiday brochure instead, from when we went to Dorset. Funny how things turn up like that.\n\nBut, sorry… your question. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.\n\nBut wishing I wouldn’t wake up… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve had that as a clear thought. It’s more that I dread the day starting sometimes — the kettle, the letters on the mat, trying to remember who’s meant to be coming round. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.\n\nI suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your wife since her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. It’s hard not to be. Since her fall, everything in the house seems to point back to her — her medication chart on the kitchen table, the cushion she likes, even the little notes she used to leave by the phone.\n\nI used to be the one with lists, you know. I had a plan for everything. My old students used to tease me about it when I was mentoring — “Have you got a spreadsheet for that?” they’d say. And now I just sit there staring at that chart, and I’ll either start crying or snap over something ridiculous, like a bill coming through the letterbox.\n\nSo yes, I’m preoccupied with her, but it’s not just thinking of her fondly. It’s like everything’s got stuck there, around the fall, and the rest of life — bills, calls, the washing-up, the hoovering — it all piles up because I can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t weighed myself. We’ve got scales somewhere, I think in the bathroom cupboard, unless my wife moved them—she used to tidy things into places that made perfect sense to her and none to me.\n\nAs for noticeable weight… I can’t really say. My clothes haven’t shouted at me, if that makes sense. I’m not suddenly swimming in them or anything I’ve properly noticed. But I haven’t been paying much attention to myself, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I couldn’t honestly say it’s been significant, no. I haven’t put myself on the scales, and I suppose “significant” sounds like something you’d measure properly, doesn’t it?\n\nI mean, my belt’s the same hole, as far as I’ve noticed — though I’ve had that belt for years, leather all cracked near the buckle. My wife always said I should buy a new one, but I never liked shopping for that sort of thing. You go in for one belt and come out wondering why there are twenty types of black.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed losing a lot of weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly.\n\nThere are times I’ve sat there and thought, “What’s the point of all this?” You know, the house is so quiet in the evenings, and the clock in the hall sounds louder than it ever used to — we bought that clock on a day trip years ago, actually, she said it was ugly and then wouldn’t let me get rid of it.\n\nBut if you mean have I properly thought about not being alive… I don’t know. It frightens me a bit even hearing it said like that. I haven’t really… I haven’t sorted it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I think that’s fair to say.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve just not been myself. I used to be the one with lists, plans, little systems for everything — bills in one folder, calls written down, shopping done on Tuesdays, that sort of thing. Now I sit at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and just… go to pieces sometimes.\n\nAnd then other times I snap over nothing, which isn’t like me. The post piles up, housework piles up, things I said I’d do for people — mentoring calls and that — I just let them slide. So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… “lost interest” is a tricky way to put it.\n\nThere are things I haven’t done, certainly. I used to fuss over the garden a bit — not properly, mind you, more moving weeds from one place to another and pretending it was horticulture. My neighbour once told me my roses were “brave,” which I’m still not sure was a compliment.\n\nBut whether that means I don’t care about things anymore, or whether I’m just… distracted by everything since her fall, I don’t know. I still notice things. I still think I ought to do them. It’s just hard to say if the interest has gone, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser on you than they were before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, to be honest.\n\nI haven’t noticed my clothes particularly hanging off me or anything like that. I did once flood the airing cupboard trying to be useful, which she never let me forget.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve really paid attention to whether things are looser. I haven’t noticed it, anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your wife since her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. It’s hard not to be. Since her fall, everything in the house seems to point back to her — her medication chart on the kitchen table, the cushion she likes, even the little notes she used to leave by the phone.\n\nI used to be the one with lists, you know. I had a plan for everything. My old students used to tease me about it when I was mentoring — “Have you got a spreadsheet for that?” they’d say. And now I just sit there staring at that chart, and I’ll either start crying or snap over something ridiculous, like a bill coming through the letterbox.\n\nSo yes, I’m preoccupied with her, but it’s not just thinking of her fondly. It’s like everything’s got stuck there, around the fall, and the rest of life — bills, calls, the washing-up, the hoovering — it all piles up because I can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t weighed myself. We’ve got scales somewhere, I think in the bathroom cupboard, unless my wife moved them—she used to tidy things into places that made perfect sense to her and none to me.\n\nAs for noticeable weight… I can’t really say. My clothes haven’t shouted at me, if that makes sense. I’m not suddenly swimming in them or anything I’ve properly noticed. But I haven’t been paying much attention to myself, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I couldn’t honestly say it’s been significant, no. I haven’t put myself on the scales, and I suppose “significant” sounds like something you’d measure properly, doesn’t it?\n\nI mean, my belt’s the same hole, as far as I’ve noticed — though I’ve had that belt for years, leather all cracked near the buckle. My wife always said I should buy a new one, but I never liked shopping for that sort of thing. You go in for one belt and come out wondering why there are twenty types of black.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed losing a lot of weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly.\n\nThere are times I’ve sat there and thought, “What’s the point of all this?” You know, the house is so quiet in the evenings, and the clock in the hall sounds louder than it ever used to — we bought that clock on a day trip years ago, actually, she said it was ugly and then wouldn’t let me get rid of it.\n\nBut if you mean have I properly thought about not being alive… I don’t know. It frightens me a bit even hearing it said like that. I haven’t really… I haven’t sorted it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I think that’s fair to say.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve just not been myself. I used to be the one with lists, plans, little systems for everything — bills in one folder, calls written down, shopping done on Tuesdays, that sort of thing. Now I sit at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and just… go to pieces sometimes.\n\nAnd then other times I snap over nothing, which isn’t like me. The post piles up, housework piles up, things I said I’d do for people — mentoring calls and that — I just let them slide. So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… “lost interest” is a tricky way to put it.\n\nThere are things I haven’t done, certainly. I used to fuss over the garden a bit — not properly, mind you, more moving weeds from one place to another and pretending it was horticulture. My neighbour once told me my roses were “brave,” which I’m still not sure was a compliment.\n\nBut whether that means I don’t care about things anymore, or whether I’m just… distracted by everything since her fall, I don’t know. I still notice things. I still think I ought to do them. It’s just hard to say if the interest has gone, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser on you than they were before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, to be honest.\n\nI haven’t noticed my clothes particularly hanging off me or anything like that. Mind you, I tend to wear the same old jumper most days at home — the blue one my wife always said made me look like I was about to fix a boiler, though I’ve never fixed a boiler in my life. I did once flood the airing cupboard trying to be useful, which she never let me forget.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve really paid attention to whether things are looser. I haven’t noticed it, anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t really keep up with those commitments. And it’s not just that. Bills, little jobs around the house, keeping things ticking over — I used to do all that without even thinking. Now it all slips. The post piles up, something needs fixing and I just… don’t get to it. Even ordinary routines at home have gone rather badly off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been keeping track of my weight. The scale’s in the bathroom cupboard somewhere, unless it got moved when we had that leak under the sink — awful business, water everywhere, and the plumber was late by hours.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a significant change. My clothes… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. I just haven’t properly checked.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t quite know how to answer that neatly. It’s all been rather overwhelming since the fall, and some days my head feels full of fog.\n\nHarming myself… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made any sort of plan, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I just want everything to stop for a while — the forms, the appointments, the ringing phone. Yesterday I spent twenty minutes looking for the insurance papers and found an old holiday brochure instead, from when we went to Dorset. Funny how things turn up like that.\n\nBut, sorry… your question. I’m not sure I can give you a clearer answer than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wished you wouldn't wake up in the morning since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d put it quite that way. Mornings have been difficult since her fall, that much is true. Sometimes I wake and for a moment forget, and then it all comes back, like a weight on the chest.\n\nBut wishing I wouldn’t wake up… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve had that as a clear thought. It’s more that I dread the day starting sometimes — the kettle, the letters on the mat, trying to remember who’s meant to be coming round. Our postman used to whistle, actually, years ago, drove my wife mad.\n\nSorry. I’m wandering. I can’t really say yes to that. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed, no. I mean, I put on the same trousers most days now — easier than deciding, really — and they seem to do up as they always did.\n\nI suppose if something were suddenly loose or tight I’d have noticed, wouldn’t I? Though half my shirts never fit properly anyway. My wife used to say I bought everything a size too big because I didn’t like changing rooms — which is true, those curtains never close properly.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve noticed a change in how my clothes fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your wife since her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. It’s hard not to be. Since her fall, everything in the house seems to point back to her — her medication chart on the kitchen table, the cushion she likes, even the little notes she used to leave by the phone.\n\nI used to be the one with lists, you know. I had a plan for everything. My old students used to tease me about it when I was mentoring — “Have you got a spreadsheet for that?” they’d say. And now I just sit there staring at that chart, and I’ll either start crying or snap over something ridiculous, like a bill coming through the letterbox.\n\nSo yes, I’m preoccupied with her, but it’s not just thinking of her fondly. It’s like everything’s got stuck there, around the fall, and the rest of life — bills, calls, the washing-up, the hoovering — it all piles up because I can’t seem to get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t weighed myself. We’ve got scales somewhere, I think in the bathroom cupboard, unless my wife moved them—she used to tidy things into places that made perfect sense to her and none to me.\n\nAs for noticeable weight… I can’t really say. My clothes haven’t shouted at me, if that makes sense. I’m not suddenly swimming in them or anything I’ve properly noticed. But I haven’t been paying much attention to myself, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I couldn’t honestly say it’s been significant, no. I haven’t put myself on the scales, and I suppose “significant” sounds like something you’d measure properly, doesn’t it?\n\nI mean, my belt’s the same hole, as far as I’ve noticed — though I’ve had that belt for years, leather all cracked near the buckle. My wife always said I should buy a new one, but I never liked shopping for that sort of thing. You go in for one belt and come out wondering why there are twenty types of black.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed losing a lot of weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly.\n\nThere are times I’ve sat there and thought, “What’s the point of all this?” You know, the house is so quiet in the evenings, and the clock in the hall sounds louder than it ever used to — we bought that clock on a day trip years ago, actually, she said it was ugly and then wouldn’t let me get rid of it.\n\nBut if you mean have I properly thought about not being alive… I don’t know. It frightens me a bit even hearing it said like that. I haven’t really… I haven’t sorted it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I think that’s fair to say.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve just not been myself. I used to be the one with lists, plans, little systems for everything — bills in one folder, calls written down, shopping done on Tuesdays, that sort of thing. Now I sit at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and just… go to pieces sometimes.\n\nAnd then other times I snap over nothing, which isn’t like me. The post piles up, housework piles up, things I said I’d do for people — mentoring calls and that — I just let them slide. So yes, I suppose I do feel sad a lot of the day, nearly every day, but it’s tangled up with feeling useless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… “lost interest” is a tricky way to put it.\n\nThere are things I haven’t done, certainly. I used to fuss over the garden a bit — not properly, mind you, more moving weeds from one place to another and pretending it was horticulture. My neighbour once told me my roses were “brave,” which I’m still not sure was a compliment.\n\nBut whether that means I don’t care about things anymore, or whether I’m just… distracted by everything since her fall, I don’t know. I still notice things. I still think I ought to do them. It’s just hard to say if the interest has gone, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are looser on you than they were before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, to be honest.\n\nI haven’t noticed my clothes particularly hanging off me or anything like that. Mind you, I tend to wear the same old jumper most days at home — the blue one my wife always said made me look like I was about to fix a boiler, though I’ve never fixed a boiler in my life. I did once flood the airing cupboard trying to be useful, which she never let me forget.\n\nBut no… I can’t say I’ve really paid attention to whether things are looser. I haven’t noticed it, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your clothes are significantly looser on you than they were before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Significantly looser? No, I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed that.\n\nI mean, I’m not exactly inspecting myself in the mirror these days. Half the time I’m in the same trousers I wear round the house — my wife used to say they’d seen more history than the British Museum, which was unfair but not entirely wrong. But no, nothing’s stood out to me like waistbands slipping or clothes hanging off. If it’s happened, I haven’t really clocked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I couldn’t really say. I haven’t weighed myself, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWe’ve got scales somewhere — bathroom cupboard, I think, behind a box of old plasters and one of those travel hairdryers my wife insisted would be useful and then never used. I keep meaning to clear that cupboard, actually, because every time you open it something tries to escape.\n\nBut no… I don’t know about significant weight loss. I haven’t noticed anything definite.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
